LADIES' LETTER.

Mv DEAR NELLIE, Girls are wearing frocks with skirts fitting closely round the hips and widening out at the hem, resembling the shape adopted by grown-up ,people, more or less exactly according to the age of the young wearer. The bodice is invariably lull fronted, and has some kind of vest, yoke, or chemisette. A very pretty model made of Wedg- wood-blue voile has its skirt edged with three stitched bands of glace silk in a rather deeper .shade of hlne, and a pouched bodice fastening over to the left side with four large silver buttons. The big cape-collar is of tucked white silk edged with deep cream lace, and the heart-shaped vest is of alternating bands of tucked silk and erram insertion. This particular frock has ordinary tight -sleeves, finished with cuffs strapped with glacA, but awn-sleeves with under-ones matching the yoke -would be, of course, smarter. Another nice aress for a girl between eight and 12 is of pastel-blue linen. The skirt is trimmed down the seams with bands of ecru guipure lace and the front of the lull bodice Corresponds; the round collar is stitched aearly all over, and is edged with the lace, the small vest being of drawn ivory-white mousseline. The waist is encircled by a dark blue satin-ribboa sash with long ends. This little frock could be satisfactorily copied in almost any shade of art ,en.

I daresay you will have noticed what a common sight it is in the streets to see women tripping along in blissful unconsciousness of their skirt and body having parted company in the back. This should be given especial attention. The very best way is to gather the fulness in the back of the body and stitch over it a strong tape or piece of belting which way be tied or drawn through a small buckle in front. If drawn snugly the fulness in front may be kept in p ace also. Have the band on the skirt quite tight, so that it will beep the skirt up nicely. Draw the back of the body down under the skirt band in the back and fasten body and skirt together with two strong black safety pins, or have hooks on the skirt and eyes on the body. Flat buttons on the waist belt and buttonholes in the skirt band will also serve the purpose ricely. Pull the skirt well down in front and pin the band to the corset. This. will keep the skirt from creeping up. When these directions are followed there is no necessity for patent fasteners, parted skirt bands or hunched up skirt waists. A leather belt or one of strong material is best with such a costume. It there are no collars and cuffs, ribbons may take their place. Many women do not like stiff collars and cuffs, and when a body is made for them it appears very evenly to go about without something in she* place. A becoming ribbon around the co, k, tired Wee a bow at the back looks we,i;.

several of the new hats have perfectly flat imams, not raised in the least above the level of Ise brim. The shapes are large, and every degre• if curve is represented. A pretty hat is in white tulle, with a wreath of small pink roses, alternating with black ones all round the brim. Small flowers save succeeded the immense ones of last spring. rimy sprigs of heather, lilac blossom, or forgot-me- aot are in great favour. The Duchess of Devon- shire hat is chiefly seen in a modified form, but later on in the season there is some probability of ita being seen on some of our smart young beauties En the full dimensions of the original. Transparent sate are in again, and a large "cartwheel" Gains- borough, inblack lace and shaded poppies for trim- ming, with the inevitable oBtrich plumes, would race a charming h,

Elastic hello i. wtite and pale tints, some three or four inches deep, arc being embroidered with Rowers or Oriental work instead of the steel and jet rivets with whieli they have been studded. A flower belt is charming with a white gown. Embroidered flounces are replacing lace for full- drew petticoats, many of them being made to button on to silk foundations so that they can easily be taken on and off when dirty; thus a silk petticoat may last fresh for a considerable time with the addition of this removable flounce, whereas with the lace edgings and frills it is no longer a thing of beauty after once or twice wear- ing. For morning and country wear simple jupons in spotted batiste or mercerised cotton are being 11106 t sold; the spot is often raised in black or White flax thread, and the edge of the flounce is scalloped out and whipped over to match. Your loving Jwimru.

THE MOST NUTRITIOUS.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

It mast have been p'easant to the Duchess of Cornwall ' and York to receive news in Au•tralia of the inaugura- tion or a handsome drie4king, fountain which has been erected at Richmond, Surrey, to the memory of her mother, the Duchess of Teck —The people of Richmond and Kew, knew perhaps better than any ott ere, the worth of the late Duchess, and it is safer to believe tha the Mayor expressed their feelings when he said that the fcue,tain was intend, d to perpetuate the memory of the noblest Princess that ever lived. _

The King who is 'pending Whitsuntide at Windsor will greatly miss thc se quir t trips to the continent which he used so frrquently to enj ,y, when Prince of Wali s. I Few,among the many change following upon the death 'of our late Sovereign, are rrore irksome to the King than the knowledge that h- can no longer go about either at home, or in the foreign capitals and waterine places, comparatively unobserved. Even when the period of Royal mourning is at an cod, His Majesty will not be able to dine out among his chosen friends, or to go to the Opera, or theatre, or the races, with tb. same freedom as he need to. It is therefore scarcily surprising that the King feels that a great change haa come over his life, in which His Majesty inobably tbelgreatest relief in the &ate dutes which now occupy so much of ti,a time.

As the King's birthday clashes with Lord Mayor's Day, there is some doubt whether His Majesty will attend the Guildhall bat (suet N•sarly all cur Sovereigns have made it a point to dine with the Is' rd Mayor— Queen Victoria made no exception to this rule, and attended the Guildhall banqu t soon after tier accession to the Throne. Charles 11. used frquentty to go to the Civic Feast, and of course, in those days it was no un- usual thing for the Lord Mayer and Company to get somewhat elated with constantly toastiag he Royal Family, and other turniours. The country round Windsor, and indeed everywhere, 113 now at ita best. With the c,hestnuts in full bloom, and the fresh foliage of the trees, who could with for anything better than a ara'k or drive along the green lanes, or by the coontry side, to enjo7 the unsoiled beauties of Nature. Of all things !bat influence our lives for the beat, there is nothing s inspiring of hope as a country walk on a fine sunny day, during the leafy months of May and June, This also is the lime of yea-, when garden lovers enter upon the enjoyment of thf ir long labours of preparation. Oily those with the en- thusiasm to become their own gardeners, fully realise the satisfaction with which they watch the peepine shoots, and the opening buds, and heir gradual expAs- lion into colour and totcean in all their summer glory. The difficulty of raising the fashionable skirt out of the dust without revealing an undue amount of petti- coat has caased underbkirta to bec me as elaborate as are costumes. They are hired, tuned, mixed with lace and insertion, and in (very way show the ex Dendi- tura upon them, of many hours of labour. A French woman always has a fresh and dressy underlert, some- times white, more often of some darine tint. In this , the Britishwoman still occasionally reveals herself ; she : has the daring, tut not always, alas, the tint, Now and again harsh scarlet may be seen haneirg below Royal purple, orange frills perchance appear, under a dull pink skirt ; or green-blue flounces beneath a gown of rich dark blue. This clash of colours is truly British and, unfortunately, does uot always confine itself to onderskirts Such distressiug mixtures as a bright blue costume a scarlet sunshade, and a hat wreal had with yellow flhwers ; or again, plum-colour, mauve, and clear blue ; or yet again, a China-blue cos- tume, a crude pink lining, and a crimson parasol may not iefrequently be seen. Names of tints go for little, it is the harsh tones inartistically arranged that offend so greatly. Among the most popia'air hats for early summer wear, are those with double brims. Frequently a half-wreath of small flowers is carried round the boat between the two brims where it gives a pretty line of c( lour. The single rosette or cluster of flowers under a bat brim is 'rarely seen, the half-wreath arrangement has taken its place, and on single brimmed bats, is laid along ni xt the hair. Few girls look really well in the new three-cornered strews, which show a single red rose lucked into each of the three hallow corners of the upturned brim.

The crass for cretonne embroiders continues, and has extended now to our shoes and hose, the latest of which show ornamentation of this description. F..r ribbons, a running design is deemed desirable, and th.s is nicely workrd on with gold thread. For sunshades, which have seen their last days, the cretonne embroid- eries are without doubt invaluab!e. ta. trailing design covers the most te-tale of soiled seams, and freshens up the whole concern 'cost

Thcse who do not sketch or psii:t. yet Wi3il for a race- fill and profitableaecomplishmenr,should try pyrography or scorch-painting. This is chilly done on white wood though also applied to leather, and to textiles, and all the worker has to do ir to follow plainly natrlted outllnes with a point of metal he wed red hot. Charming erf«,ct results,and designers have, as always, a wider field than those who merely corm. Small articles of furniture and trifles enough to stock the largest and most varied bazaar, can be prepared, after very little practice, by pyrography. The modern machine is safe and effectual, and a new edition of the best known handbook has ju.t been issued to meet tta) demands of an ever-iocreasit g This pokerwork on white wood suggests sepia draw- ing, or when liked, can be colored, either whh plain stains, or with meta Ide tints that gleam out effectively and are especially WPII MAU d to tbe developments of heraldic designs. These COlf Ura on burnt leather are exceedingly handsome, ar d appropriate, and various novelties have been prepared for this season.

The question of furnishing is ti.WayB a difficult one, and up-tc-date styles are not parriculsrly pl, seine There is a tendency to revert to straight-backed, narrow, and genera;ly severe enairs, or to terrible barrel or split cyclinder shapes, with arms thas just bit the elbow of a restless occupant Rush seats and bar k•,and stained wood frarmrs, are seen in library, hall, and dining-room, shapes and materials anise suggesting those of kitchen and parlour furniture, a hundred Nears ago. 'the very clocks too have cars tb at are plain to ugliness. The old style of folding chair for occasional ure, wan of wood, with a back and seat of stretched carpet, or for the luxurious, of tapestry, or plush. Some very pretty new ones are on the lame old principle as far as collapsing properties go, but are of mahogany, rose. wool, and other ornamental timber.and when expanded display a slightly raised and comfortable seat, and a high back, with cushions to fit soothingly into the shoulders. These Challll are not so compact as the old gat type, but have the advantage of Leine safer Severe injuries have ben it flic'ed upon incautious fin- gers, whie.h were caught into the j'inte of plain folding chairs, which were not always easy to manage. The high-ba &red chairs aro of good pattern, and in the terms dear to the catalogue writer, "In ornament to any room." When rugs and fancy mate are used to cover a polished !Ix's, in halls or living rooms, they ought really f 6 be lined underneath with rubner sheeting, to prevent them aliening. Sometimes serious sec dents arise from this cause, and very often a graceful entry into a room Is entirely spoilt by unwarily stepping upon a loose mat or 114: on a slippery surface. Instead of shaking hands, one is scrambling wildly for anything to clutch hold of, to save oneself from falling. Rubber sheeting of the necessary thicknrss is, of csurse rather expensive, but it is the only way in which small mats and rugs can be made lasi, viten used on polished floors.

RAILWAY SERVANTS' SOCIETY.

Councillor Pierpoint on the Railway-

meri's Dispute.

Under the auspices of the Warringtnn Branch of the Amalgamated Society of Railway Servants, a concert was tuld in the Cc-operative Hall, Sankey street, no Monday evening, when occasion was taken to unfurl the new banner which has just been obtained. The scheme for the purchase of the banner was mooted some eight or nine months ago and it was taken up with en,hu.iastn. At the present time the cost of the banner (£45) has been pravically defrayed. The proceeds of the co, cert were in aid of. the orphan fund which was establish( din 1880. In that year 38 ehildren wese placed on the fund, in contrast to 379 in 1899, and 247 in 1900 Altogether no fewer than 2,877 orphans had been assisted. Mrs W Pierpoit t performed the ceremony of unfurling the banner on Monday evening. Councillor Pierpront presided and suppnrticg him on the platform were Mrs PierPOinr, Councillor James Evans, the Res CU Heck n, J F Marth, ws. E R Tarhuck and Me..ers J Upson, Malone and A Meer, the organizing secretary et the srciety. At the c, mmeneement of the proceedings, The CHA/R7.IAN. in the course •-,f a short speech, Sal,' he had always done what he possibly could to help the railway men of Warrington—yhear, hear)—and he was still prepared to help them. Since he was asked to pre- side at that meeting he had been appointed& member of I the Boar% of Conciliation in Warrington. (Applause) The Board had been brought into existence mainly through the instrumentality of the Trades and Labour Council—(hear,hear)—and, therefore, perhaps some ot his confreres might say he was assuming a wrong Posi- tion in taking the chkir at a meeting which was held f r the purpcse of suppnrting an orga, iz.tion ot his position as am miler of that Board he Was tr.und to Fay considerable importance to what his fellow manu- facturing brothers had to say. It would be injudielem if he did anything that was contrary to what he con- sidered was right, and therefore he made that ntn, exnlanation. UP was there t",at evening in order to show how far he w.s in sympathy with the railway men of Warrinoton in their endeavour to add to the useful- ness of the nrganizasion—the Amalgamated Society of R ilway Servants The society bad been formed for self-protec•ion—tn protect the inter sts of the railway men of Warrington. (Hear, bear.) He desired to show his sympathy with the railway servants of Warrington and all over the country. Very few men travelled over more railway lines or travelled greater distancre than he did, but. none had travelled in grater safety. He had bet travelling for 30 or 40 years, and during that time he had never had the slightest accident or the least discomfort, (Hear, hear and applause ) That was due not so much to the time tables and r;1. Way engine and rolling stock, as to the care exerc,sed be railway men over the passengers entrusted to th#l, care. "It was the evening before last that we made our escape," said Martin. "There were more than one of us, a whole gang; and there- would have been half the prisoners in the gaol in the- revolb if it had not been for one man, a huge fellow -named Hawkins, with whom I quarrelled and, fought some months ago when we were first drafted to Minewood." He pretended to take an interest in the rebellion which another new lag—a new prisoner I mean—had organised. This was a man named Minton, who had received a brutal thrashing when he went lute the prison simply because he had harboured a tiny tame mouse." • _

"What a brutal thing to. do !'" said Grace, whose sympathies were always keenly awakened by anything concerned with the dumb brute creation. "Yes, I. witnessed the scene,. unfortunately," said Martin. "Well, when the mouse was killed in his sight, Manton struck the warder who com- mitted the act—a very serious offence. He was thrashed and sent into the dark cell' for several days. What the dark cell is like Ido not know, but it is very terrible." "Thank God, you have escaped that," murmured Grace.

"Hush, dear, Ido not know. If lam captured now, it mayonean that and worse." Grace shuddered and drew nearer to Martin.

"When Manton at length came out he was hungering for revenge. By chance he was drafted into our gang and I got to know him. We became sworn friends, and I suppose it was because of our friendship that 1 found myself almost unconsciously falling into his plans for effecting an escape. He had dreadful provocation, poor fellow, I will admit, but the boldest in the prison would never hate thought of proposing such a scheme as little by little Manton unfolded. to me and to the other convicts. Wby he was so desperately anxious to get clear I did not know, for he only had two years to serve, but they goaded him into defiance, and I believe he rather gloried in giving the warders trouble. The plan was simple, yet perilously bold. It was this: We were to wait day after day, until the first fog occurred, when we were out at the works. You know that when the fog descends, as it often does at Minewood, the warders immediate'y get their gangs together and march them back to the prison, whilst the outside cordon of sentinels have their rifles at full cock ready to pick off any desperate wretch who thinks his chance has come to make a rush for his liberty. . They often try that little game on, but it is almost impossible to succeed."

"Yet you did." "So far, but wait. When the fog came, there was to be a given signal by means of a long-drawn whistle from Manton, and in that instant the con- victs were to make a united stand with their picks or shovel S or whatever came handiest, beat down the warder in charge of their respective gangs, and make a wild dash in the fog for their liberty. By escaping all at once it would be impossible for the sentinels to pick off everyman with their rifles, and ttns the great majority would have e ~.thance." We waited for several days, but no fog fell. Then one evening just as we were about to return .to the prison a thick mist dropped over Minewood, and in less than three minutes everything beyond a hundred yards of us was blotted out. "My nerves were tingling with excitement, for I montantarily expected the signal to run. I expect the other men felt the same. All of a sudden Manton, who had been working by my side for the greater part of the day but had been removed to Ak quarry a short distance away, gave a shrill whistle. In an the gang 1 was in was on the offen- sive, and rushed at the warder in charge. So completely taken by surprise was he that his truncheon was snatched from his grasp and he was lying stunned on the ground with a blow from it, without making any but the faintest resistance, and without giving the slightest alarm. THE LADDER OF GUILT.

Br ITLYSS ROGERS, . Author of "For Love and the Queen," "Wedded

CHAPTER XVI. THE FACE AT THE WINDOW.

At the sight of this sudden apparition, a falai scream escaped from Grace's lips. "There Look there!" she cried, pointing to the window.

Low, arrested half=way across the room, turned. suddenly. But quickly, as he had faced about, the man at the window had moved more quickly. There was nothing—nobody to be seen. "What is it ? " •Isked Low. "I see nothing." But Grace had sunk back into her chair, her face deathly pale, her hands clutching,•her breath coming in gasps. For even as she had cried out in alarm, she had recognised that face at the' window. Those terrible death-haunted eyes, those famine-stricken, emaciated cheeks—she knew them, changed though they were. The man at the window was her lover, Martin Fenwick, and she, in her nervousness, oh, what had she done? He was a fugitive, and she, who should have screened him, who should have held her peace, had been the first to give the alarm. . Her father, at her cry, had come running back into the room. He looked sternly at Low. "What is the meaning of this?" he asked. "I do not know any more than you," said Low. "Miss Hendon cried out that there was something on the other side of the room, at the window, apparently. I turned to see what it 1/118, and found nothing." "It was nothing," said Grace. "I only fancied I saw somebody. But it was Mr. Low's shadow on the blind. I'm afraid I'm getting dreadfully nervous. I am so sorry, Mr. Low. And father, too—l'm afraid I must have startled you." But neither of the men was satisfied. Farmez Hendon remembered hearing once from his .dau,ghter's lips of this same Douglas Low attempt- ing to kiss her against her will at the farmyard gate, though that had been forgiven and almost forgotten bug ago. This incident brought it to the farmer's mind. 'Had he, against her will, offeree her the insult of another kiss He resolved t 4 remain in the room the rest of the evening. And Low, too, was even more suspicious. For he, being an observant man, had noticed that in the position in which he stood when Grace had first called out it was impossible for the fire to cast hit shadow on the window. He had been upon the point of suggesting that they should settle the question by opening the door and looking, out, but he was a very astute man, and his craft hinted at a different course. It was, perhaps-, as well to allow the old man to hug his delusion, or to accept Grace's explanation. For himself, he would know, inde- pendently of them all, what had transpired outside while his back was turned to the wintiOw ; and, knowing, he would act on his own initiative, with'. out being under any obligation either to keep or divulge a secret. _ _

- "It is Christmas Eve, you must remember, Mr. Hendon," said Low, with a laugh. "I'm afraid Miss Hendon has been saturating herself with the overwrought sensations of the usual seasonable ghost stories. This strong firelight dbes cast strange shadows. I don't think rn stay any longer. Why, bless me, how the time has gone I Eleven o'clock! A good night's rest will steady your nerves, I trust, Miss Hendon. Good-night. Good-night, Mr. Hendon. Don't trouble; I can let myself out. It's snowing still, I see. Good.-night." They heard him as he stepped out through the paved porch, and presently the outer gate clanged, an intimation that he had gone. But in this they were mistaken. He-had banged the gate loudly in order to deceive.

He passed down the road a matter of twenty yards until he came to a disused barn. The snow muffled his footsteps, so that it was impossible for anyone who had been listening to gain any guidance from them. Once in the shadow of the barn, how- ever, he went no further, but nimbly ran round to an angle whence he could command a view of the house and, despite the still falling snow, set him- self to keep watch. The reason for these unexpected tactics- on the part of Mr. Douglas Low was obvious. For up the road and along the track to the house were foot- steps ttha had not been visible when Low entered the • farmhouse earlier in the evening. Low had seen at a glance that they had led to the window, and that they proceeded no further. Judging by the tintertangle of prints, the man who had caused them had doubled at this point, and as was clearly indicated, had passed the door again and made his way round to the back of the house, where he was without doubt still in hiding. Who could this man be? Low racked his- braine to think of some other suitor who should be as much at home at Fenwick Barton as to come to do his. wooing near the hour of midnight. But his mind could fix upon no one. Burning with jealousy and curim.ity, he resolved to•take what appeared to be the most convincing course—to wait in hiding and see what would happen. He had not to wait long, for in less-than five minutes the farmhouse door opened, and in the red light stood Grace, waiting at the door. Then it had been acting, after all, that make- believe faint of hers and that repudiation immediately afterwards. As he suspected. • Now, who was the man? AS if in answer *to his thougtds a crouching figure limned itself black against the whitened out- houses; a tatterdemalion figure, without a hat, and in clothes that hung in shreds about him.

Then a cry, a -cry of joy, rose on the air, and Grace flung herself into, the open arms of the stranger. He held her there, clasped close to his heart for a moment Then he lifted her into the house and the door was shut. Who could it be? A mad, ungovernable anger seized upon Low., He clenched his fists until the nails dug into the palms, and brought the blood. He had been so near, he thought, to winning his quest that night. Was she playing, fast and loose with him? He had never heard of another lover being in the field. Then another, the correct suspicion, crossed his mind. But, psbaw ! that could not be entertained for a moment. It was too impossible. There was but. one way of making sure, however, and that was the • same that the stranger had utilised in taking his survey of the interior of the farm. It needed no second prompting to induce Low to, put this sug- gestion into practical effect. Rapidly he retraced his stops, and on tiptoe made his way over the snow to the house. The window- blind still left that vacant space in the corner. He waited a moment, then carefully, little by little, he drew nearer to the uncovered pane until just one • eye gained an „insight of the ffrelighted room inside. Only one second did he look, then eapain, and anger. defshot eyes, with a haggard he fell back nilto,h7hbiir close cropped, and cheek- !, suddenlyuFnothunted d aezp;wmitnhssialov bones that threatened to burst their way through the flesh, so starved was he, sat in the farmer's chair by the fireside. And round his neck were Grace's arms, and hot tears of great gladness were pouring down from her beautiful eyes upon the shrunken-trembling hands of the visitor. Douglas Low did' not, need to look twice. That man was his own cousin, the respited man, con- victed of murder, Martin Fenwick. With a low, --fiercely muttered curse, Low, hardly knowing what he did, dragged his limbs, trembling with excitement, to the fartnhonse door. There he hesitated a moment.. . "Fool, fool that I' should be," he said. "It would undo everythrng were I to enter." He turned and took to his heels, running down the path. Be reached the gate, opened it, took care to close it with absolute noiselessness behind him, and made off down the read at full speed. . A short distance from tha farm the road forked, one branch going to the village, the other leading away further into the country past the wood in which the Parrs lived. He rounded the road that led back to Fenwick, but when he had gone thirty or fold), yards he suddenly pulled up. "What am I thinking about? " he said to him- self. "My brains seem altogether to have left me. No! Not I, but Fan'. He's the man to do this." He ran back to the corner and then diverged as fast .11,4 his legs would curry him in the ' direction .of Parr's cottage.

CHAPTER XVIII. A SAUD MAIM

"I suppose I was the best sprinter, for after a few yards I found myself leading. Then out of the fog came a shot, another, and another. They were fired at hazard in the direction we were heading, but I heard one poor fellow behind ma give a groan and stagger, sprawling to the earth. I ithetald like to have stopped, but I heard a voice belaind me. It was Manton's. •

"'Don't stop,' he cried, hoarsely. They are just behind.' "I looked round and could see half-a-dozen strange shapes in the murky darkness of the fog. Oneln convict garb was not more tharesix yards in the rear, and I knew by his gigantic form it was the prisoner with whom I had fought,. Hawkins. Behind him I could just recognise the parti- coloured costume of a convict who had been in

disgrace, and I guessed instinctively it was Manton. Still further back ran another convict, and in the rear some fifty yards or more were two black% shapes that I rightly guessed were warders. "Even as I looked round one of these two stopped short, and,' raising his rifle, fired again. The hindmost convict fell like a log in reaponse.to the shot, and the warder who had fired called' out to, se to stop or be would servo us the same."- "' On, on,' cried Manton. Run zigzag and they can't hit you.' I took his advice and' dartedt away to my right, then back to my left in short,. sharp tacks. But I had scarcely gone half-a-dozen yards thus, when I felt a heavy weight on my back, a pair of huge armsowere thrown round my neck, and, by sheer strength of brute-force, I was forced: backwards to the earth.

"It was Hawkins, who had served me this dastard' trick.. The fellow was spent himself, perhaps, or felt it futile to rue further, and to gain a pardoui , for himself had resolved upon my capture. " This'll pay off the old score, I hope,' he. , hissed in my ears as we lay on the ground. "The words were scarcely out of his lips when ai heavy blow fell upon his face, and there was Manton kneeling over us, and digging his teeth into the giant's big knotted hands. The brute released his grip at that, and struggled to get• to his feet to fight or wrestle it out. But. the blow Manton had given: him left him half-dazed and sluggish, so that when he half rose he fell back, again. • "Manton's arms were under m 8 shoulders, and, • with one lurch he had lifted me to my feet. Not a. moment to soon. The warders were but twenty pm*, away, coming down the slope at a heavy rush. Away we darted, running zigzag fashion as before. "'l'm done V I heard a warder cry. 'After them. while / fire. I can't run another step.' "A shot whizzed past me a moment later, and . them a, succeeding one followed so quickly as almost to appear simultaneous. I heard Manton .give a sharp cry. " What is it ?' I asked. • Are you hurt ? ' ".4.Not much,' he said. Only hit in the hand. It was a near squeak. I think we had better divide. ' You go to the left; you will reach the creek another , mile and a-half further on in that direction. I: will go to the right. Good-bye. God help you.' "He had darted off even while he was speaking. 'Good-bye,' I cried into the darkness, and that was the last I heard or saw of the truest, bravest fellow

• I have ever known." "And did they leave yon then to chase him?" asked Grace, anxiously, to whom the whole story. . seemed even then to be in actual progress. "No; they did not. At least., the warder; *Maker, who was personally responsible for Manton and me kept on my trail. He hated me, I knew, and no doubt put me down as the instigator of, the revolt. At any rate, he knew it would fars, ill with him if any of his men escaped, for th., Governor would be fined five hundred pounds on my account, and would retaliate on him. So I' daresay he thought that as Manton was wounded, ' he was a pretty certain capture, and left him to • his less athletic mate.

"And you, Martin?" asked Grace, who had no ears for any diversion in the story. "I ran right on ont of the fog, into open day.- . light, such as it was, for the shadows were closing in over the country. He stuck to me another half- mile. His gun wanted loading, and he was afrai& he should lose ground hopelessly if he stopped to put in the cartridges. But at length I drew so far ahead that his only chance was a shot. I had turned my head occasionally to look at him, and I saw him stop. Then I commenced to zigzag again as Manton had advised. He fired twice, and. missed me each time.

"What a dogged fellow he was! He recom- menced the running again, settling down to a good swinging trot, keeping me in sight, in the hope, perhaps, that some rustics might happen along who would assist in stopping my flight. He must have been a splendid long-distance runner in his youth, • but he was cumbered with too- much flesh and heavy clothes, and, in addition, had his gun to carry. "At length I came to the creek Manton had spoken about. What to do I did not know other than to swim it. Yet before I had got safely up the other bank, Whitaker would be up, and would, have a good chance at me. In the rapidly-closing gloom, however, I might just escape, or I might, perhaps, double down the bank of the stream and elude him that way. "I resolved to try this course first, and started away to the right, when suddenly, as though it had been planned by Providence, I saw something that I thought would aid me in escape."

(7h be continued.)

TRE FREE CHURCHES.

"ONES, OF BOURNEMOUTH." • annes, of Bournemouth," has within the iie,.st three years become a familiar name in the tee Churches. The pastor of Richmond-hill 'nngregational Church, Bournemouth, is now beginning to experience the tender mercies of the saints. Dr. Berry once said, "The tender Illetnies of the wicked are cruel, but the tender kercies of the saints are much more so." He Was referring to the crushing demands that the Free Churches make upon their best preachers in" speakers. Mr. Jones has now reached the ars* " rank, and is asked to give many more -erninns, addresses and speeches than come Within the compass of mortal flesh. Ile is Only thirty-five years of age, and has doubtless a great future before him. An interesting of his career appears in the Free Church Ciirohicie for June.

Theic chief obiection is that it pro- SOS to liana over the edneation of the country in the f Ith:re to bodies which are elected for quite different work. It does not seem to them wise to entrust the training of our future citizens, t,be 'nest vitli interest of the Commonwealth, ‘o bodies which are elected to look after drains nI ditches and the operations of the steam- `'ller• The bill seems to be a concession to clerical hatred of the School Boards. Even the I/ulie of Devonshire is beginning to resent the 4-7f:zesslcm party. Free hurchmen as a body are in favour of ad hoc ethlcation authorities and the establishment of Ilniversal School Boards.

-IR. COMPTON RICKETT'S MUNIFICENCE. , Mr. Compton Rickett, as is well known, 18 a munificent supporter of the Free Churches and Free Cnurch Institutions. Recently he erected a Mission Church at Chiddingly at his ewn exPonse. Chiddingly is in Sussex, not far from .11r. Rickott's beautiful country house at ' cathlY• The Church has now been vested in tns Sussex Cuanty Congregational Association, 'vho have appointed an evangelist to take charge of it. Mr. Rickett takes a keen inte- rest in the scheme of the National Council for Providing a Free Church evangelist within „,three miles of every village in the country. .I:his scheme will probably first be worked out ill the county of Sussex. Mr. Rickett has pro- toi give all the help he can. THE PRESBYTERIAN AND THE VICAR.

The other day, Dr. Hanson, the popular Pastor of Marvlebone Presbyterian Church ad- Itessed the communicants at St. James's ehurch, Holloway, at the invitation of the Rev. ,Tarcus Rainsford, the Vicar. it is doubtful Whether such a courtesy has ever before been 'tended to a Presbyterian minister by a Vicar the Established Church. Dr. Hanson, who .6.lleeeeded Dr. Pentecost, is a handsome, cut.. 7etl and refined Irishman. His cotagreestion t°,l4hins some of the most influential men in coclldon. During the Simultaneous Mission he b.lldUcted a campaign along with the Rev, 'Ugh Price Hughes at Brixton. Dr. Hanson' LBl?ealte in quiet tones, full of expression, and as "Is discourse proceeds his keen intellectua face glows with passionate earnestness. He is particularly strong on the temperance ques- tion.

"HOW LONG IS IT TO CONTINUE?" Dr. Waller, at a meetiang of the Wesleyan tiucation Committee, called attention to a

case of Anglican intolerance. A girl, who was a 'Wesleyan, was engaged as a candidate in a Church school, and at the end of the year she Wished to be apprenticed. The clergyman consented, but he shortly afterwards died. Ilie successor declined to allow the girl to Proceed unless she received the rite of Con- firraation. This is only a specimen of what I:,aPPens not infrequently all over the country. 'nrely, it is a grievous wrong that Noncon- forraists, who are half the nation, should be ex- cluded from the teaching profession in schools t° which the nation supplies the bulk of their funds. Happily, in this case, a British school Iwas near, to which the girl was apprenticed. The education authorities recognised the year Previously served in the Anglican School. , TUE ILLNESS OF REV. BERNARD J. SNELL. The news of the serious illness of the..popu- lar pastor of Brixton Independent Church : has been received with profound regret throughout the country. Mr. Snell is suffering from enteric fever. It comes as a climax to a series of calamities. Some months ago Mr. Snell lost his daughter, and since that time his wife has had most indifferent health. Mr. Snell has had in consequence to be removed from his home at Streatham to a fever hospital. He is quite conscious and Cheerful, but he will probably be in the hospital for fully a month yet. Mr. Snell pos- -4esses a powerful frame and constitution, and 11,as hitherto enjoyed excellent health. It is 'toped, therefore, that he will be able to escape, ,Ic a large extent, the terrible weakening and "ePressing effects of the dread malady. A BOOM IN FREE CdURCH PUBLICATIONS.

The Publication Department of the National I .90uncil is enjoying unprecedented prosperity. Itiless thane month, a largo edition of the Free ITurch Year Book has been nearly exhausted. 4 The Call of God," the volume of bimultamous Mission Sermons, is now far on in its second E'dition. Within two months 35,000 copies of .4 All Things New," advice to new converts, by Rev. G. Campbell Morgan, have been sold. -clae Free Church Catechism is now in its 21st edition, making a sale of between 300,000 and copies. 45,000 copies of "The Story Gipsy Smith's Career" have been sold. The ational Council will shortly issue a manual on 13Pen-air work written by the Rev. F. 13. Meyer, .A.

MISSION VANS. The National Free Church Council has Solved to purchase ini,sion vans for work in l'ne rural districts. The men in charge of the v,ftria will act both as evangelists and colpor- r,eurs. There is plenty of room for an enterprise of this sort.

AN ACADEMICAL OUTING.

Members of the Warrington Society

_ at Rostherne.

The headline ib ove is founded tin the orssump- tion that aaything which emanates from the academy ma,t necessarily be academical even though a casual oeserver might not think so. Baturday's wend; would nor, strike the man in the street ss having any features about it which were noticeably academical. Neither woull the savoury odours which—sometimes permeate he pellucid atmosphere at Bridge Foot between the hours of twelve and two. Yet bo:h emanate, or have emanated, from tlte A .a'letny, and are therefore academical. We need nitrify mention that the members of the Warringten Society have their head- quarters at t. is ancient, honoured building, for the fact is fairly well-known tat they a:e the only people its Warring in with academical pretensions Where those pretensions sometimes lead them, lo,wever, we are not at present going to discuss, " suffi.oent for the day is the evil thereof,' and On Saturday thr academical pretensions were left at h. m-t,tis is customary on at least one occasion in every year. T. at occasion is the annual outing, when the archaeologist forgets for the nonce to muse over mystic marks in the mud, the lilts ateur forbears to pursue his cafiap yrative analysis of toe works of John :quart. Mill and W W Jaeobs,and the other "curs" enl "'oloeists" all divert teeir ardent enthusiasm from the passion play and snakes to the prospects of the picnic, which, for the time being, are equally enthralling. The average man likes a picnic, and the same may be said of the ordinary academician, his li fry ideals notwith- standing. 14ot:rover he possesses a capacity for e. ejovine himvelf while engaiee picnicing which is not exceee ed even by a street Arab. It is not, therefore, eilEctili to imagine or b4ieve that what we have boldly styled" an academical outing was en exceediagly enjoyable event, and one that will cause last eaturday to be a wed-reimembered day. At or about two p.m. the boozed c matenance of Oliver Oromwed might have been observed to relax from its aecust ome severity of expression as the merry picnic party foregathered in his broad -halow, half of them with bicycles and the re- mainder wit h a well-found .d claim to at least 18 inches sLace on the seat of a "brake." Fortunately for them the allowance of spaca was rather more liberal, There was a very full master of " bikists " and " linkers " and the parry, aeout, 40 strong, set out for the very pretty village of Rostherne, prompt to time. When atehforil had been left behind the total had swelled to filty,fully half of whem were of the gentle st•x. The academical mind has, by the way,a distinct leanieg towards the ladies, "who do cheer but not inebriate," as one member oecasioa- ally recites, It is a beautiful ride to Rostherne, I especially if you can persuade oteier people to amble serenely through the dust you unavoidably thr iw up But when it is your lot to plug through the chokey cloud someone else is raising, then the by becomes tempsred, By preading over three or tour miles of road, however. nearly everybO ty succeeded in escaping what might have been a real drawback, or the "dry spell " had coated the splendidly-smooth Routsford highway with a velvety suifade of dazzling white, and proved pungency. The country looked its best in the glorious bls,zis of sunseine,trees anti meadowland displaying every shade of green, varied here and there with golden buttercups and, in shady places, a profusion of wild liyacin, he. Half-way en route the cavalc de branched off to the right and t iok in a view of Svvitteyard Hall, a chatm:ng old country farmhouse, with a broad and well-filled moat still encircling it Beyond this little diversion the journey out was devoid of incident, but brimful of enjoyment. Rostberne was reached in good time, and well did the fir e view from the churchyard repay all for the visit. We never remember to have seen the mere and its wall-wooded slopes look lovelier. There was a richness of colouring in water, sky, and land almost Italian rather than English, and few places could have taken the palm for sylvan scenery from R eitherne last Saturday. After feasting on the view for a while the party be- came prosaic and marched off to feast on something more substantial at a farm about half-a-mile or less from the village. It was a merry meal with quips and jests flying about in choice profusion,and after- wards there yetis more than an hour to spare for the purpose of rambling. Seven o'clock was the time for re-assembling and by a quatter past the hour everybedy was pursuing the home- ward route, which was plsasingly varied by Way of Tat:ou Park and Mere Corner. Warrington was reached soon after nine o'clock and the "Aca emical Outing" was at an eml. Very en- joyable bad it been, and we are pleased to give this testimony to the fait that the Academicians lack nothing in the way of humour, j dlity;or good j fellowship when once they emerge from the sombre shadow of the statle.

The usual fortnightly meeting of this Bard was held at the Workhouse Dutton, on Tueoday last. Admiral J mfiP. rry, io subino,tins the Stoup° Own- mittee rep, rt, referred to the question of the School Children's Lurch. It appeared toot the children were provided with two ounces of "s -ed cake," and were in the habit of eating it at school. Be did not propose to say much ! abut it at present, because the inquest was not over, , but he would brmg it in again, as he believed they would have something to learn from it. The Late Rev James Warnock. Mr TS Steel the Bolicitur to the es: of the Rev James Warnock, who was for over 23 years the minister of the Presbyterian Church in Winwick- street, has just recet d a copy of a minute. unani- mously aoiopted by the Synod of the church held at Regent-square Church, bond n, on the 3rd inst. The memorial minute referred to reads as follows: " Ordained to the ministry, in connection With this Church at Viiarringt.to in February, 1877, the Rev James Warnock, M.A., 8.D., pro: ecuted his entire ministry of over 23 years in that cAugrega Ardently devoted to &tidy, fie gave him-elf ts, the companionship of his books The fruit of this patient and loved toil tq,pettred in his conscienti- ,tously prepared and carefully delivered sermons. Of a very retiring disposition he shrank from intruding himself inio general slciety, but among his vwn people, especially in their times of sickness, or . sorrow. he was always attentive. considerate, and tenderly sympathetic. In the public life of the town,he took but a small share, tough as a member of the Free Church Coined and in other respects he rendered services which were highly prized. Both by his own congregation and by the community at large, Ins memory is cherished as tha'.-• of an upright and blameless man, and a humble and consistent Christian. Pis brethren in the Church realize that in him they have lost a brother of singular modesty whose life made continuously for kladues4 and peace."

THE LATE REV. G. S. REANEY.

'Tribute by the Rev. J. Yonge.

The Roo. John Yonge pal I a fitting tribute to the memory of the late Rev G S Heaney n Sun lay, iii the Course of his meriting sermon at Wcliff Couch. The disc-ore' was based on the text " I must work the woik, of llen (Mat sent in., while it i. day : tee nieht nometh wnen no in.in can work," John ix., 4- verse. Tars ter'. op said, ever gain', d new force from fresh eveet. Only a few weeks aeo did its teachiug come ns wly to hie we heart when his old comrade and trisnd. Urijah 'Femmes, of Bristol, suddenly laid down and died. in he old cellsge days, they had cenopenied togeth le tidied together,prayed in fellowsh,p, me finally a arid for :he work ol life on the very same dss . Nearly of anaete they had both been upheld in the Mister's service, teen he heard the summons and soon departed to nis rest, La t So-day news was brouelet as they were est.erneeng for ceasing that one who in nis early ti ars WAS assecia- ted with Wycliffe, had suddenly ended his career on the other side of the Atlantic. at the COrtlearetively early age of 49. He was at Wychffe Church out a few m tens aeo, on holiday, in full vigour and seemingly with years before him. But a tow brief days ot sharp illness and ha tersetned his last. feat was a message for those of his own age—Peter Shaw," being, dead yet speak em." And last Thursday there came not only to him, but al.se to In any of them, a further reminder that the span of life is brief and that the nieht comes when the work is over. The newts of Mr Rsaney's death, following in a few 11 ours their fi:st intim stion of his illness, would have affected all who knew him, and aff cted with peculiar power, many at Wycliffe who knew him when he was in W athlete°. Knowing how great'y he wig admired, stearnel and even loved by large numbers of his people at Wy cafe— many of whom still remaieed —he was not at all astoni<hed at the deep feeing which his derh had aroused. Nor 1073 S he su•prised at the geserous and gleivinetributes te:bis memory that appeared in the local papers of the:previous day. There could be no q legion that the years of his ministry in Warringtou were wonderfully successful in many ways. He came toVi'ar- rington in the days of his early vigour and freanness,and he came in suscession to an aged minister who retired from AC lee service when he left that ehurch.flis bri.ht- neas an I energy,his inventiveness and wealth of resource, his personal charm in converse and fell° wship,his popu- lar talent, his broad views and tendsr sympathies, his vivacieus baoyancy which captivated the young, added to manifold other gifts, In do him a man of mark and a minister of influence and power. Seidel aa Mr Heaney was by enthuiiastic c.- workers of beth means and in- fluence, he and they ga teree a haul of workere that in those days carried al' before them, Congregationalism speedily helan to live in Warrington where before it had only existed. Soon the stela ml biLlings hal to be en- largsd and then the first Wycliffe Churce had to go under similar extension. Tee numb e. of scholars mul CS:Med with wonderful rapidity, and the congreeetion followed suit, the membership in the church doubled in his day. Speedily it became eviden-, that the enlarged chapel was still inadeqeate to the growing demand. Tto.reepon followe 1 the great erownine work that must over be connected con- sp,cu- usly with his pastorate. Wycliffe Church was resolved upon : erected, opened, and p'rid f ire and the denornina on thereby came in' o possess on of a sanctuary worthy of is traditions, and into a position of nremi- nence and power in the town. His own personal knew- ledges of Mr R anev besets whee first he Caine thersona was larger and closer before he (Mr Yonge) becem his sue-lessor than it had been at any period since. When Mr Raney was at Warriugton and he (Mr Yongs) at Heywood, they were often closely identified in we k for the County Union ,and they met constantly in the frater- nal of the Manchester Congregational Board. He always found Mr Heaney singularly interesting. Ho was ever a large centributor to the brightness and the profitable- ness if any gathering of his brethren. He was earnest in his devotion to his mimicry, and held wile views of the sphere of the true minister's service. Be (Mr Yenge) conceived so high an idea of his gifts and devotedness that very considerable hesitancy to step into his vacant place made him (Mr Yonge) pace' for long ere he ven- tured to do sco; Mr Heaney himself was a very infl cutlet factor in leading him to accept his vacant place, for when the call came to him (Mr Yonge) it was CO Mr Heaney he went for some of the earliest advice, and it was given in a letter which he still preserved and valued. It looked now a long way back to that surnmsr of 1876. made memerable to both himself and that churcn as the time when the call from Wycliffe reached him. Since then Mr Heaney had exercised his ministry in varied surroundings. He had been known to Bay that his happiest years were passed" at Warrington and he (Mr Yonge) supposed it was true that his most loyal and lasting friendships were made and still continued here. Mr Heaney had been is a series of conspicuous positions in Congregationalism, and he made himself a power in them all H bscame especially prominent in the Cone gregational Union for the courageous way in which he befriended the toiling, ill-paid working classes in the East end of London, for his persistent exposure of the starvation way 55 paid to matchmakers ani seamstresses. The " Bitter Cry of Outcast London." entered into his very soul, and he devoted himself without a int to ameliorate social and labour conditions, still largely continued, which were a scandal to Christianity, and a menace ta the welfare and safety of the State. Mr Rowley exposed himself to considerable criticism,and no little ob'equy by that unselfish championship of the weak and nelplese. But, however open to question might have been some of his methods, and deoateable I some of his statements and arguments, no one could doubt the honesty and unselfishness of his spirit and aim. Along that line of social and industrial reform , lay a path of christian enterprise that needed persis• tently to be pursued: Concerning the last ten years of Mr Reaney's career, in which he passed from Co ogrega- Um:trillion to the Anglican E iteblphineat it was not easy to speak. To most or all of his friends it was a mnss regrettable exchange. That he took the sten in loyalty to what he believed right and true, none of them, he hoped, would desire to question. All the same, they regretted it, and many of them felt it to be a matter ths,t they could not comprehend. It could never be easy to explain so complete a reversal of life-long habit of thought, of belief, of practice, and it could hardly be wondered at that its occurrence should have occasioned a criticism that often verged towards censure. That his transference occasioned so much surprise, and awakened so much criticism was a tribute to the worth of his personality. There had been men whe had "gone over" whose departure from Free Church ministry had been only a relief. When M- Heaney left them It was a matter of very wide regret. It was a further tribute to his power that, unlike so many of the few who " passed over," he did not pass out of sight. He made his mark very speedily, even within the pale of the establishment and it had its own degrees of interest that the once Dissenting preacher, who, so largely el helped to give Congregationalism its position here, had stood in the pulpits of St Paul's aed Westminster Abbey. But now, his course was ended,his work was done, his" day" was over. Speaking from that pulpit at his (the preacher's) recognition meeting in December 1876, Mr Heaney referred to Christian work. He spoke in his own racy and characteristic style mingling many earnest csunsels with flashes of genial humour, and also with touches of real pathos. He said how he thought in every Christian Church there should be not only its workfulness, but its rest- fulness, and he said " we must remember that there came with honest toil the afterglow of evening. with sunset light from God and the sound oc evening bells, and after the spring time, and the summer season there came the rest of the harvest, the gathering in and the reaping." Should they not hope that when his evening gathered round him so quickly the other day he had some of that " Sunset lignt from God ? " Be had been an earnest worker, sowing his seed by many waters, ever seeking to live to bless and help aud cheer his fellow-men. Assuredly, he had not lived in vain. There were those in Warrington who blessed God for the good he brought them. In R -ading, Stepney, Man- ceester, Greenwich, there must be very many more. Depend upon it, in those hours when the lights el earth were last fading, and the dawn of heaven was just breaking, his heart would csme upon a sweet and precious rest and peace ttr . YONGE sueeested that the congregation should in- struct him to forward a letter of Sympathy to Mre Heaney and family. The congregation signified their approval of the suggestion by standing. BOWLING.

Lancashire and Cheshire Association. First Round —Club Championship. Warrington North v. Stretford. This tie was played on the greee attached to the hos- telry of the Cemetery Hotel, Harpurhey, on Satur- day. the turf was in fine coalition and the bowling throughout the afternoon reached a high s'audard of excellence. Tee Warringtonians in their opening essays did not fare at all well, and the decisive defeat of E Butterworth was anything but a promising start. Drink water played an exceptional'? fine game, and was practically unbeatable. The hon. secretary of the association, E Taylor, surprised his admirers by the beautiful lengths he delivered in hiCgame with F Charnbers,who is recog- nized as one of the Stretford Champions. The players peeled at the 25th end, but from this point Taylor did not &low his opponent another chance- Another fine game was tnat intween H Williams and 0 Kulow, and the bowlers several times peeled during the progress of the 26 ends, at the close of which the Stretfotd representative had won by two shots. Some of the leeway was made up by T Ellison who defeated 1 J Johnson by five points after leading at one period I of his game by eight points. Thus at the end of the I first four games Warrington were in a minority of I twelve points, T Bolton soon placed a wide margin between himself and his opponent J Twiss, and it looked long odds on the game being called 21 —4 when Twiss was lucky enough to strike and knock out a " block toucher." This opening gave Twiss his opportunity and he ran his score to 15, before Bolton was enabled to call " game " J Ashton made short work of his opponent, R D Lowe, and though the Stretford man 'leered two at the first end he practically never had another chance, and there was more merit in Aehl:on's hollow victory, than appears on the surface, for his bowling was one of tile features of the after- noon. A 3 Jones accounted for his opponent in excellent style, winning by six shots, but a lengthy game between .1 Allison and 3 Valentine re- sulted in favour of the last-named by a single point. With three substantial a ins in the second quartette the Warringroniana now had a lead of seventeen points. The issue was however, in the balance when G Ball early succumbed to J Whitehead by eleven shots, but this deficit was counterbalanced when T Bridge settled the pretensions et J Hamlet in summary manner by ten points. R Anderton made a plulky fight with S Alsopp but suffered defeat by four shots but J Harrison after being in arrear in his game with b Wigglesworth secured the sack and taking his opponent on a tickle mark scored a highly meritorious victory. It will be seen that the Warringtonians won seven games out of the twelve played and the victory is highly encouraging in view of the gre,t interest tam its this year's competition. The Rev Theodore Bishop (Wesleyan Sup,rintendent) moved the following resolution :—‘i That this congregation in the Parr Hall on Sunday afternoon, May 19th, heartily approves of the Sale of D ink to Children's Bill now before Parliament which prohibits the sale of intoxicat- ing liquors to young people under the age of 16 years.' He considered 16 years of age quite young enough. If be had his way be would raise it from 16 to 66. Then th y would have a sober country. That afternoon as he walked from Latchford he saw several young childrea carrying jags. It was sometimes said that these children were carrying milk. (Laughter). He had a peep in the jug and he saw that it was the

wrong colour for milk and moreover he could smell that it was beer. (Laughter ) Was it possible that these young girls whom they saw carrying it got to like the taste of beer? They became accustomed to it and when they grew up many of them were unable togive it U. Ha would ask them to protect the children,uand if they passed the resolution he hal proposed it would strengthen the hands of thos, WhO had tbe mem: ure in hand, lie was told that in Manchester alone "the trale " was employing 200 persons at five shillings a day—that was £5O a day—in getting up petitions against the Sale of Dtink to Children's Bill. The debate on Sir Hi LSO The Finance Bill. leowLea's amendment to the second reading of the Finance Bill ended much more successfully than the earlier course of the de bite promised, ard the Government majority of 177 was no great triumph considering the fact that tho Lishmen walked out. There were a few Liberal abstentions but these were fully compensated by the large number of Government absentees. " The latter may probably have resulted largely from the near approach of the Whitsuntide holidays, but in many cases it may be safely assumed that the determination to leave the Government to look after itself was the result quite as much of an uunmfortable feeling among its supporters that the charges of reckless extravagance rested on only too good grounds. It was well for the Liberals that it was so, because it may teach the Irish members that their irreconcilable and too independent tac- tics are not such as can bo accepted by a self- respecting party with which they may wish soon to ally themselves. The attit tide tak3n by sir ROBERT REID in the debate on Tuesday night waS that which has been advocated in these columns, and which came to be adopted as the proper atti- tude of the rank and file of the Opposition, as well as among its leaders. It is that quite apart from the question of special war expenditure there is sufficient material for dras tic criticism in the inordinate increase that has taken place in recent years in most of the leading branches of the public service. The two simple facts that the pro- bability is that the future will see not a reduction, but a further increase of the national burdens, and ' that this present and prospective increase is ' collateral with a decline in trade, constitute to- gether something of a national crisis. It would indeed be strange if a united Liberal Party had nothing to say in such a crisis. Unfortunately, as Sir ROBERT pointed out, the House was not in possession of the details necessary to afford a close examination such as might determine where economies might be effected. Ho favoured the appointment of a committee—not a standing committee such as the presont Committee of De- fence which recommended increased expenditure —but a periodical committee appointed every five or six years to scrutinise the accounts of the Navy and Army and report where a decrease might be effected. The greater part of the increase has been quite recent. During the last sit years the Army vote has risen from 181 to 30 millions, but ten millions of this increase belong to this year. There can be no doubt that these figures indics to the rash action of 11 panic-stricken Governlc-nt with all the waste that accompanies such acto n. As Sir H Cam?' BELL BANNERMAN pointed out in closing the debate, the revelations made to the House a few weeks ago that the Law Officers are now being paid thirty thousand per annum instead of nineteen thousand which was their former allowance,provos only too clearly that the same lavish extravag,anee is to be found in every public department at th° present time. Against these home thrusts Belzoni: irade an utterly ineffictive reply during the ten minutes' speech in which he do-ed the de' hate. He now begins to conce,vo as a possibilitY • that the Liberal Patty may return to power in th: not distant future, and his deMaration that %lb% they do so they will confirm and homologate ro that their predecessors have done is scarcely t_n_ kind of argument likely to carry weight in present crisis. couutry ia the

thitte.e ars sorry that the House of Commons C orn- s has refused the petition of the Blackpool to grant the power to magistrates %ester son for fourteen days on convictions for %ttlDg. They have, however, adrattted.tha ..."1411 for more araatie pnaisbaaerit by raising

the in imam penalties for first, second, and third : 'Ten,. to £5, £lO, and £5O respectively. They !h#. _ st as well have legalised imprisonment, for past experience proves that fines for betting are practically no deterrent,and by the bookmakers themselves are oftener looked upon as a tax more than anything else. Municipal authorities are almost powerless to prevent the evil, which goes on no matter how often the police prosecute. Very frequently the person convicted does not pay out of his own pocket the sum he is mulcted in, but with the assistance of a " club " can afford to smile at the law and all its works. Street betting is one of the worst forms of temptation which assail the lower classes, and effects not only men, but boys, women, and girls, who are dazzled by the prospect of the gain. H ad-earned wages soon dwindle if this form of sport is indulged in and much misery is entailed upon all concerned except the bookmaker who fattens on the spoils and lives a life of comparative ease. The operations of these social vultures can only be restrained by making the game not worth the candle. Fines are a failure, but 11 yrisonrnent " without the option" would, we imagine, deter all but the hardiest " bookies " from plying their evil trade.

The Irish language debate in the House of Com- mons revealed Mr. Wyndham in his most sympa- thetic mood to the Nationalist members. As the descendant of a famous Irishman, he was able to understand the aspiration of the Irish to maintain choir ancient literature. In some respects it was creditable, though it lacked sincerity. Not one- third of the members who were clamouring for the maintenance of Irish as a living language could speak it. But oven in the transition which most of the Irish have made to English they keep up forms and idioms which are essentially their own, remnants of the speech which is perishing. We can understand tleir desire that the fount should not be allowed to dry up, though the claim they put forward for a hi-lingual system of education throughout the country was preposterous. Dr Rentoul pointed out that all those nations which recognized officially more than one language were low down in the grade of education. Moreover, it is certain that if the poorer people were taught to speak in- terchangeably two languages they would incline to the Irish, and lose facility in the one which could help them most commercially. It would become part of their political creed to eschew English and hold to Erse, just as the Hungarians endeavour to preserve the language of their land. It deters visitors, for even at Buda Pesth they , profess not to be able to talk in any other tongue, solely in order that they may not, lose ground. Nevertheless the Nationalists have a right to say that where Irish is dominant, and the children think in Irish, they should be taught in it. Any I other course is unfair.

Before disparaging remarks. are made about amateurs in cricket, and insidious comparisons drawn between the " expenses of the gentleman and the " wages" of the player, the peculiar cir- cumstances of the game should be considered. it is highly unfair that one standard of amateurism or of professionalism should be made for all sports, regardless of the varying demands that are made upon the time, and even upon the purse, of those who practise them. It. is easy enough to make I fun of the amateur who is given a lucrative posi- tion in some business establishment, with but nominal duties to perform fr his salary, on ' condition that (hiring the summer months his services shall be at the call of the County Committee. It is easy enough, too, to deride secretaryships and assistant secretaryships which are given not by one county alone, but all over the country, to amateurs whose service is chiefly rendered neither in the office nor in the' committee room, but in the field. It must be re- membered, however, that playing in first-class cricket means hard will< from the beginning of May to the end of August, a demand such as no other sport makes upon its vJtaries. To insist that a cricketer should either be an amateur as rigidly defined as the swimming amateur, or as the National Cyclists' Union endeavours to make the cyclist, or else perform the duties of a ground bowler for a weekly wage would be to cut out absolutely a class of players who really make cricket the attractive game it is. The true I question is whether the present system is inatru- mental in maintaining a high level of cricket, and though possibly the social distinction between gentleman and player might on some grounds be made less marked and though some minor reforms might be suggested, vet on the whole the answer cannot be otherwise than in the affirmative. mmaamelepoommlNl.

MATTERS OF MOMENT. _ A Provincial Letter.

Whitsuntide is again at hand, the" feast of glad- ness," and it. remains a time-honoured custom _ in the provinces to devote the f€ stival to rest and re- creation Most people have some kind of holiday in Whitsun week, and just now the townspeople are either packing,or already flitting to the country,the sea, or the lakside, by rail, "bike " or motor as the case may be, All that is enjoyable in these excur- sions, it is 'rue, depends very much on the weather, and the weather has not yet learned obedience to the Astronomer Royal, or to the enterprising pro- phets who are ready to go bail, as every morning's D per tells us, for its behaviour. This little draw- back, however, has not deterred the Meteorological Office from announcing during the week itswilling- ness to supply forecasts of weather by telegraph to thos . whom they may concern, and who are ready to pay for them during the next harvest time. Granted one or two fine days of good weather after to-day there is no reason why, despite continued fighting in South Africa, this Whitsuntide should not be celebrated in such a way as to be a refresh- 1 ment to the bodies and minds of hard-worked men and women from John o' Groats to Land's End. Lately it has been diflicult,judging from superficial appearances only, to tell whether we were in May or March, though the weather has been mostly fine and bright. Persistent east winds, felt chiefly in the morning and evening, made Spring a delusion, and the beginning of the " merry month " a mockery. Yet Hawthorne had the courage to say, as the reader knows, that the one thing of which Friglishmen ought in reality to feel most proud was their clime e; so, perhaps it would be judicious and prudent to wait awhile before grumbling at the arrangem-nt of the season. There is only too good reason to believe that, as I the census re urns have been demonstrating, the country has in r-cent years become less attractive 11 to its own people than it was, say in the old days I of Maypoles and morris dancers, of wrestling II matches, and Whitsun ale, when it was one of the I customs to appoint two young men in every parish to collect funds fom the obliging parishioners which they expended in brewing and baking against Whitsuntide," and when at Vie festive season the neighbours met together in a great barn, called the "Chu ch-house." and there m.rrily fed on the victuals they hid provided. Besides which, says the historian, the neighbouring parishes did make loving visits to one another, and in this way frankly sp nd their money together;the af ernoons being passed in sue exercises as oil and young did. most enjoy. The Church looked on at these popular pastimes with approval, not to say encouragemen, ; which was perhaps natural, inas- much as the profits from the consumption of Whit- sun ale went to repair the" outside "of the sacred building, or for such oher nharitgble purposes as the rriests might desire. The times since then have changed indeed. Instead of " frankly spending their money together " in their native village, these people now-a-days hie away at Whitsuntide to the towns end eities eta diPtance,there to indulge 1 their eves in sigheeint.r, and their minds in con- cocting plons to get to and live there permanently.

In this case, however, it happens that something like half a million schedules have to be examinsd and reported upon, so that the delay is fairly intsllig qle. With- out. going into all the fivires relatin4 to the withdrawal of cultivable laud on the one hand, and the exten don of a few millions of aeres on the other, suffiLle it to say that the net gain in the regularly farm ..d land of Great Britain amounts to more than two million acres, chiefly accounted for in the first twenty years of the period mentioned and due, it is reported, partly to the closer collection of informa- tion for the later permi c mipared wito the earlier returns, and partly to "tie steady encllsure of pastures not originally regarded as cul!i% ael per- manent grass." A survey of the whole periolliince these returns were fir t collected shows that in the latter third of the nineteenth century the land uheler wheat in Oreat Britain has elin.el from 3,688,000 acres in 1860, its maximum, to 1,845,000 acres in 1900, and that a few years ago wnen in 1805 the minimum was reached, our wheat acreage fell as low as 1,417,000 acres.

From questions asked in the House of Commons this week and the answers given by the Chancellor of to Exchequer, it would be gathered that the Mining A-sociation ol Great Britain,at least, had no idea of allowing the agitition against the proposed, export tax to drop. By what transpired however it appeared that members of the Associat on, and some of the branches, were not in a-cord with the parent body,and did not approve the resolution adopted by the latter to the effect that the money should be raised this year by a tax on the who'e output of the Kingdom for last year and that an impartial tribunal be appointed to repo-t, on the advisability ol an export •ax on coal, its incidence, and its probable effects upon the coal trade and the miners. Meanwhile, Mr Arthur F Pease has in the " Times " ben urging that the tax should be ad valorem, and pointing out that there has been an enormous fall in the price ot coal since last year. As an instance of this, he men- tions that for unscreened cooking coal, during pa-t of last year, it was possible to obtain 184 per ton f.o.b. on the Tyne, whereas recently the :Arne coal has been sold for Os Gi a ton. Mr Pease has a right to be heard, and his views have additional weight from the fact that when the war expenditure was discussed he recognised that the coal trade last year was well able to provide part of the money, and suggested a 10 per cent, ad valorem luty on all coal exported. If all eoal owners had taken an equally patriotic view of the subject,there:might have been no such agitation as there is now in the coal buoi ness.

Of course, the Bishop of Winchester was disap- pointed with the atnendm-nts proposed in the House of Lords by Lord &Apr relative to the Licensing Sessions Bill, but his lord-hip had less reason to complain of the action of the Government with respect to the Ifsintual Drunkards Bill. still it does not matter much whether the Bill is a Government measure or not, so long as the law is altered to meet the case of habitual drunkards, and those people in the provinces who take an interest in this numerous class are glad that the Bishop's Bill has been accepted, rat ler than the subject should be shelved for another Session. The Rome Secretary having ben unable to introduce his Bill, in the Lower House had less 'esitation in approv- ing the Bishop's measure, with certain alterations. From a temperance point of view there will be very little direct Rain, however little or much may be expected to accrue indirectly, but little as it may he it will be regarded in tiese days of small conces- sions of this kind, as better that nothing at all.

After the drink question comes betting. Mr Justice Wills recently stated that for a long period he had regarded the former as the greatet of these evils, but that in later years he was inclined to regard it as a neck and neck race between the two. When the Bishop of Hereford introduced his motion this week in the Upper House, for a Select Com- mittee to inquire into the increase of public betting Lord Salisbury hesitated to follow with any great diffidence the suggestions contained in gpfser,he4 delivered by the mover. by tle Bishop of London, and by the Archbishop of Canterbury, but, as on previous similar occasions, he did not oppose the appointment of a committee, which was eventually agreed to In face of the ever-increasing number cot complaints heard in many large provincial towns and cities, where public betting is regarded as a positive and dangerous nuisance, this, too, will be accepted as an agreeable concession in its way.

For the first time S.x)tlond has a larger popula- boa than Ireland, a tact that we may expect to bear a great deal of when the question of Parlia- mentary representation comes to be canvassed and discussed. But as in England and in Scotland, it is only the towns that increase in population, at the expense of the rural districts.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A COIIIIRSPONDENT.]

With regard to the licensing prolecutian which took place at Runcorn Seisions last Monday, I although no conviction followed, the police ate to be very highly complimented for bringing the matter forward. Juries have held inquests time after time upon the b 'dies of sailors drowned in the Canal as the result of drink, and in face of the strong rider they added to their verdict, in the case of the man Tucker, although as a jury they could not bring the blame home to anyone, it is still satis- factory to see that the police took up the matter, and although unsuccessful, the result of the action will be that, the licensed victuallers of the town of Runcorn will bear it in mind and profit thereby.

It is pleasing to learn that the Library will in the future be opened on Tuesday, Thursday, and Satur- day afternoons, from two to four p m, in addition to the usual times of opening, and that the Library Committee have off4r3d the post of Librarian to Mrs Jones (the widow of the deceased librarian). The arrangemsnt is a &atisfactory one and one that meets the public wishes in every respect.

The townspeople are patiently waiting their opportunity of shouting ones more in honour of our returned Volunteers, and are eager- ly enquiring what, the Chairman ot the Urban District Council has to propose on their be- half. They knim that the matter was left in abeyance until his return, but he has been back sometime and the muoh desired public meeting has not yet been held. That it will very well stand over till after the hoiidays is probably the excuse. but what with the holidays and the rent collec^or there will be little enough left for our soldier citizens.

The Runcorn Guardians have been having a little dircugsion about some seed cake supplied to the scholars attending the Dutton School. The matter is a paltry one,and ought never to have been touched upon. Just because some unfeeling and unthinking children have complained to their parents that the Workhouse chil t has been given seed cake for lunch, and those parents have complained of it; the Guardians now propose to make the children eat their " two ounces of se.d cake "before going to school, and thus defeat the very object of the lunch.

A new banner. recently purchased by public sub- script on from a well-known maker, will be used to head the proces.den of the scholars connected with the mission on Whit Mend iy. It is of beautiful design, and is perhaps equ il to any in Runcorn. The ground work is of royal blue, and the lettering is worked in gold, tastefully shade i with dark colour- ing..Enscrolled across the top of the banner are the words " Hope Hall Sunday School," and in the c ntre is worked a pair of clasped hands, indicative of the right hand of fellowship. Underneath this fitting motto is a picture—wrought in beautiful colours—of a guardian angel protecting a little child who has carelessly strayed into a perilous position. Underneath is the very appropriate inscription "The Lord shall guide thee continu'lly." The , inverse side is equally interesting, being a perfect' study in colours. It is occupied entirely with a biblical scene which shows C'lrist blessing the Jewish children, the picture lying interpreted by the text "Suffrljttleclijl,jrpn to come unto Me." In aid of the mission a brass band has been recently formed and it is eonfilectly anticipated that the first Whit-Monday in connection with the mission will be an auspicious occasion.
