The Sequel to a Sailor's Death.

The Mathematical Witness.

Thomas Whitfield, landlord of the Queen's Hotel, Runcorn, was on Monday last sued at the Runcorn &salons, before Messrs AR Norman and .1 Pritchard for unlawfully permitting drunkenness on his licensed pre- Mille on the 28 It of April, and aleo with serving drink to drunken persons. Mr W Piotcher. solicitor, Northwich, acted for the pronecution, and Mr W Smith. barrister. Liverpool instructed by Mr Knowles, solicitor, Widnes, appeared, for the defence.

Mr W Fletcher, in opening the case, eaid it was pro- posed to preceed with the first charge, as they did net know how far the second summons would be effected by the decision on the first. The circumstances of the lease related to the night of the 28th April when a man named Tucker lost his life at no very great dirance from the public house, and as the result of the Mile 38t facts came to the knowledge of the police which resulted in the present action, and as it was thought their wor ships might have an opportunity of judging whether the delendant was ta blame, and whether he was answer- able in law for the condition of the men which was sup- posed to have resulted in the accident of the night. It seemed that on the night in question O'Hare and a man aimed Tucker, both firemen, who arrived at Runcorn tne previous day, went ashore between 7 and 8 o'clock p,rn. They preceeded straight Co the Qaeen's Hotel. Both mon were quite sober when they entered the house, but kit it in a very (11T:rent con- dition. Tucker had unfortunately lost his life, but O'Hare would be called and would tell the Bench bow they left. He was sorry to say, but it seemed from the tune the men entered the house up to the time they left, they were carousing all the time. They were set- ting themselves out simply to get drink, and had the landlord exercised anything like viligance be must have known weat was going on. It was unfortunate that three of the men wile were summoned as witnesses had lett the town and would not be present. He submitted, ia the face of such a condition of things, that a sub- stantial penalty ought to carry if he established hl % case. Henry O'llare,fireman, of the s.s. Trafford, Lampeter- road, Cabbage Hall, Liverpooesaid he arrived in Run corn on April 27th. The vessel was tied up at the tidal basin. On the Sunday night following he came ashore with Patrick Tucker, the second engineer. They went to the Qa..etea Hotel direct from the ship and got there between 7 and 8 o'clock. They remained there up to closing time 10 p.m., and were drinking ale. He had over five pinto, but hew much over he did not know. He and Tucker were drinking " big gills," when he left the premises he was drunk ; and, so, too, was fucker as far as he could see. He was served by the eefendant Whitfield. A number of them went out together at closing time, and he remembered crossing the read. Afterwards his memory was a blank until he came to:some timelater,when he found him+, If knocking at the door of the lockmen's hut. He was subsequently taken to a doctor's, and attended for an injury to his back. He did not remember anyone having a drink,bat toe did sem a bottle outaide.—Orrae-examined : He did not remetnber falling,but only thought he had fallen,for his back was hurt. There was no row in the Queen's Hotel that he heard of, and he and Tucker left very quietly. He had 21 when he went into the public home, but %gum he came out he had nothing. When Cantain Williame, his skipper, asked him where Tucker was, he told hen " he must be behind drinking with some men who were drinking out of a bettle." Williams asked " whether he W:l9 sure ?" and he replied " He must be." Mr Smith : 0, you inferred it? Witness: Yes. . . .

John Michael Cain, chemical labourer, 11, Dale-street, Runcorn, &Nil he se w several men corning out of the " Qtvion's " about closing time, and they appeared all rignt.—Crns.-examined : When he went into the Qu ,en's Hotel he was quite sober, and if anyone told mm he was drunk he would have said they were telling lies.—Mr Smith : Is it habitual for you to lose your memory P—Witness : Yes, sir —(Laughter). It often occurs with me.

Robert Pierce, labourer, of Nelson-street also swore to being in the house. He saw the rism leave. Acton and Jones were three-parts drunk, but the two sailors were quite drunk. Cross-extmined : All he could swear was chat Ar:ton had the bottle when he crossed the road. There was no row or qoarrelling amongst the men whilst he was present. —Re-examined by Mr Fletcher, witness gave it as his opinion that Acton and 'his friends were drunk—. There were some thref—parts drunk, and others quite drunk. (Laughter.)

• P O. John Shaw,of Runcorn, who was on duty on the 28th April in Waterloceroad said as far as ha could see the men were walking straight and quietly.—Pressed by Counsel, be would say, that when he came across O'Hare at 1-30 a.m.,o'flare was drunk. This concluded the case for the prosecution. For the defence, Mr Smith contended that even if he left the case as it was, he had no hesitation in saying there was not a court, or jury in the ciountry which would convict on such evidence. Criticising the svidence, he contended that two out of three of the wit- nes.ses alleged a most wonderful lapse of memory, which in face of the tragedy which had taken place that night was most remarkable:Three others of the witnesses who had appeared before the Coroner were missing, having entirely disinpeared for reasons best known to them- selveS, a circumstance which was most disappointing. But with regard to the witness Pierce, he was what he would call a mathematical witness,dividing drunkenness into half, three parts, and quite drunk. (Laugher.) happily the law held no such distinctions, and he aub- ' mitted that the prosecution had totally failed. Their defence would be an the defendant had stated to the police when told that he would be prosecuted, "that the men were sober, and they would bring plenty of witnesses to prove it."

A LANDIIA RIK ALREADY.

The year 1901 has already become a landmark in the history of the United Kingdom, and sorrow has marked with unwonted universality the whole of the nations of the Earth. How two is it, that grief and affi ction are the common lot of at, ar d that suffering c ones to all men and women alike, the great and the lowly, in the palace and in the cottage ; but in sickness there is the consolation that sufferinK may be assuaged, strength increased, and health—generally sneaking—restored by attention to the dictates of Nature, and the enliatmen• in man's service of the bountiful goodness that abounds in the fertilty of the earth, the gifts of a beneficent pro- vidence. The most striking instance of the gathering In of these benefits for human suffering is found in Gwilym Evans' uinine Bitters, wh,ch comprises a scientific compounding of the virtues to be derived from Batas p tr- ills. Gentian, Burdock, Lavender, Dandelion, Saffron, and Quinine: an array of health giving tonic proper lea not to be surpassed, even in Nature's Garnery of Good- ness. This reviving, invigorating,strengthening remedy, when.otber attempts to cure have failed, often brings quick'irelief to sufferers from weakness of both, and un- strung nerves, indigestion, poor blood,depressed spirits, and it increases the joy of life to all. WARRINGTON PARK.

Some of its Interesting Features.

Warrington does not possess— many attractive ! features—its most devoted itenairere will be ready to admit that—but one thing it can b ast of, and that is a fine public park. It is doubtful whether ' many towns of the size of Warrington can claim to be ahead of it in the latter respect, and Warring- tonians have just cause to be proud of their park. Situated in the heart of the town it may ha likened to an oasis in the desert, and no doubt it is the means of briatening the perhaps mdnotonous lives of many of the inhabi- tants. For what can be more pleasant alter a day's bard toil, than for the workingman to spend an hour or so amid beautiful surrounoings, where nature's handiwork can be seen to advantage, de- spite the fact that outside the park the hum of business goes on unceasingly throughout the day. At the present time the park is well worlh a visit. The sunshine and showers of the past few weeks have had a visiloy good effect. The trees are in full leaf, whilst the laburnum and lilac and a dash of brighter colour to the folage. Durittg the past week the vane-coloured tulipe have been the source of admiration- by the visitors, but it is in the course of the next week or two that the gardens will be looking at their best. The rhododendrons will then be in full bloom, and an excellent display is anticipated The trimly kept grounds speak volumes for the painstaking care which has been bestowed upon them by Mr Parker, the head gardener, and his assistants. The aviary, which contains a splendid collection of birds, is now undergoing its annual cleansing and lirnewabing. One of the most attraotive features of the Park is the aquarium which was recently erected. It comprises eleven large tanks, wich contain Loch Leven (British) and Rainbow (Californian) trout, carp, roach, orff, loach, eels and tench. It was indeed a happy idea when the Town Hall, Park, and Bathe Cornm•tt.ee decided uperi this addition to the Park. What before was a b ank wall now pre- sents quite a rusticated appearance. Bark end ' cork have been utilized in coveting the brick., and these have been r ,ughei over with cement. The tanks are placed in ucesses and the ptetty effect is heightened by the insertion of flowering plants in niches brined for the purpose. The fish seem to thrive well amid their artificial surround- ing and their movemeots are daily observed with interest by large numbers of visitors. By far the most interesting aequisition,however. . is the trout hatchery at the res.^ of the aquarium For the informmon of our readers we may state that the public are allowed to inspect the hatchery on apolication to tee Park Keeper (Mr Ashton)who is willing to supply any information concerning the breeding of trout. The department well repass inspection. Here may be seen something like 30,000 Lech Leven and 30,000 Rainbow trout in .the course of hatching out There are several troughs containing the fish in different stages of growth. In the course of two or three months 39,000 of the trout will be sent to the rearing ponds in the Bollin.

The Serpentine and small ponds are well stocked with trout, and one cannot help but contrast these fine larg-i specimens with the small fish in the hatchery. At about six o'clock every even'ng the trout are fed, and the feeding i indeed a sight to remember. A representative of the 'Examiner" watched this interesting operation on Thursday evening and was astonished at the voracity of the trout. It may seem strange, hut nevertheless, it is a fat that when the Park keeper approached the water carrying a box contivning the food the fish imme lately commenced to swim rapidly towards the feeding place, When the meat was thrown in, the usually placid water was all astir, the trout throwing themselves bodily to the surface in order to obtain their share of the morsels. In the space of a few minutes very little of the mat was to be seen. The "Examiner " reporter gleaned the intere.it- , ing information that the whole of the trout, both in the winds and the hatchery,' consume between them eleven pounds of meat daily. A short time ago an experiment was tried, to see what quantity of food the trout really could demolish, and in the course of fourteen days the fish consumed the hindquarters of a cow in addition to the daily allowance of eleven pounds of meat.

From the foregoing it will be readily conclu led that Warrington Park is a credit to the town, and in regard to the pilcatorial department, it is ques- tionable where any similar parks in the coumry can equal it.

SCHOOL ACDOMMODATION IN WARRINGTON.

More Schools Wanted.

The school accommodation question in VI erring- ton is becoming a sedous one. With a steadily in- , creasing population it natu-ally follows that the number of children seeking admission into the pub- elementary schools increases proportionately. , Since the census in 1891 the increase in the p,pu- lation of Warrington has been at the rate of 1,0001 per annum and judging by the commercial pros- perity of the town there is ground for the belief that this rate of increase will be maintained. The problem to be solved by the educational authorities in Warrington , is how to keep pace with the demand for school accommodation. lu large centres where a school board is in existence, the problem is easily solved by the erection of new schools at the expense of the ratepayers, but in a town like Warrington where no such educational authority exists, the difficulty is not so easily disposed of. The burden of responsibility ress upon the managers of the voluntary schools and at the present moment they , are sorely perplexed as to Vie steps to De taken to ' meet the pressing needs of the town. The Education Bill re•:ently introduced into Par- liament does not help them in the least, but on the contrary tench to create a feeling of unrest as to , the future control of voluntary schools. The pro- posal to place higher grade and technical schools under the control of the municipal authority gives colour to the rumour that in the course of a few years, elementary schools will be placed under the supervision of the same au!hority. Such a con- summation would be heartily welcomed by many }educational reformers in Warrington, and would solve the difficulty which now confronts the volun- tary school managers, for one of the first duties which would devolve upon the Corporation, as the educational: authority, would be to build new schools.

In the absence of any such authority the diffi- culty has to be faced by the voluntary school m ,n- -agers and at the present moment the matter is receiving serious consideration. The number of children for whom accommoda ion is provided in the elementary schools of the Borouvh is 13,461. At the present moment there are over 13 000 chil- dren on the school registers,'the averag- attendance for February last being 12.926. It will therefore be seen that there is an urgent need for steps being taken to provide additional accommodation. The matter was pur very succinctly by His Majesty's Inspector, Mr Phelp-, in the following letter addremsed to the Rev ti ,rley Stevenson : " West Kirby,l2th December,l9oo Dear Mr Steven- son.—The ad litional accommodation needed in Warrington comes to raner a la-ge order and is, as far as I can judge, somewhat a f Alows :—(t) en- largement of St Ana's national (for which plans were, I believe, prepared nearly two years ago); (2) enlargement of Sr Barnabas' national infants' de- . partment ; (3) enlargement of Latchford St James' C E iniants% or, -better still, a new infants' school and the present room thrown into the mixed school; (4) a new school somewhere in the district at pressnt insufficiently supplied by the Fairfield and Hamilton-street schools. Of course, I can only form my opinion from the crowd- ing and refusal to admit which I find in the existing schools. I kn tw nothing of the children—thouith I nave no .I,,tib^, that there are many—who are not attending school at all. A really satisfactory estimate' could only he obtained by a census of the children taken by the local authorities"

The only extensions in pro4ress at the prpsent moment are floe in connection with ilver-street

The Moderate Drinker—One of the Stumbling

Blocks to Temperance.

By invitation of toe Upt; elandriand of Hope Union, the Lanca4pre and Cheshire Band of Hope and Temperance Union held its quarterly meetings at urrell, near Wigan,on Saturday, the proceedings taking place in the C ingregational Grerch. Mr Jacob Eernshaw, chaiT man et the Union, presided at the meeting of the Exectr ive Committee, and amongst those present were ‘ir Henry ehornton, Warrington ; Mr J Walkdeo,Hlossop ;Mr T E Halls- worth,hori s cretary; Mr W Chandos W risen. eecret ary ; and Mr A-pinall,president of the local union whilst„representative,• weee present, from Man chester, Li, erpool, Salford, Pr eston, Lancaster. Bl..ckburn, Balton, Barrow, Accrington, Southport Stockport, Hyde, Color, Rochdale. Ashton, Macclea- field, Chester, Nor hwtch, C, ewe, Wigan, Warring- ton, and other towns. In the evening, a public festival was held in the Congr.gational Church, Orrell. Mr T Me:Mug (Parbold) presided.—The Chairman remarked that the Temperance outlook was brighter than it had been for the last twenty or thirty years, and on every hand they had indications which should fill temperance workers with the greatest en- couragement. He believed that. before the present session of Parliament terminated they would wit- ness a dis: inct advance in the right direction. Mr S Pope, jun., ot Manchester, said that the moderete drinker was one of the main stumbling-blocks to progress. If tied° in general was properly subject to law and regulation, then the people who made the law were entitled to decide what things mtgli, be safely sold, and in 41-Tiding that they were al-o bound t consider not wily tr.s or that particular sale. but what might be the effect of a g.-neral, free. and open sale as rega; ded the whole community ; and, secondly, if the arrangements for obtaining drink were such that evil was produced, then thr, existence of toe evil which followed conferred the right to interfere and restrict. Hi+ point was not whether the sale to the moderate drinker was going to do any harm, but wh-ther there was danger attending the general liberty to perform that act a the sale of alcoholic l'qour. It was on that they had based the claim for interference. The law did not allow a man, however moderate a drinker, to get his liquor when he likd. 9 tea they iound that injury flowed from a licensed house to the cene- munity, then he said the moderate drinker had no right under the cireumstauces to force on the com- munity the maintenance of that house to satisfy hip own ccnycnience.

THE EXAM EN ER FRI AY, MAY 24 1901.

LATEST NEWS.

THE DISASTER TO THE NEW SHAMROCK. The King in Peril.

The Amerin Cup challenger, Shamrock 11., which was to have undergone important trials on Wed- nesday with the King on board, was caught in a squall and reduced to the condition of a wreck. The King, although placed in imminent peril, happily escaped entirely unhurt. The Central News yesterday morning was offictilly informed that the King is (pito weh. and was not suffering in any way fr ,m the ace: lent. Rig Maierity will, as previmisly arranged, take Dart in the trooping of the °lours on the Horse Guards Parade to-day. Numbers of telegrams have been received at Marlborough House, congratulating the King on his escape. At the time of the accident he was sitting on the companion steps, and a sailor in avoiding the falling gear almost jumped upon His Majesty.

Liverpool Volunteers Leave Warrington.

The Liverpool contingsnt of Volunteers, who arrived in Warrington on Friday evening from South Africa, on their w.ay home to Livprolot, left Warrington on Satur- day tiftertonn. About 4.30 the men mustered on the square at Orford Barracks, and about 15 minutes later marched to the siding of he Cheshire Li(l93 Railway Company. in Battersby-lane, where a spesial train was waiting to convey them to Liverpool. A large crowd bad assembled, and as the men marched past, cheers were raised. After the train had steamed out of the siding the crowd quickly dispersed. At Liverpool thd men had a moat enthusiastic receptidn.

Aify dear--, As we are now rapidly approaching Kimberley, I am' writing this letter in great baste in order to pest itthere. I expect you will be very surprised to hear where I am. i We reached Capetown about 4 a.m., Tuesday, the 23-d,l it being quite dark and the town lightrd up. Wc, anchored a.id waited for the dawn. schools, and taese, when c.nnolsted. will give accommodation for aoout 200 ad litional children. This is but a small matter in comparison with what will be required in Warrington within the next few years if a school board is to be averted With the exception of the schools in Town IHall Ward, which are in the centre of the town, all the schools are filled to ov3rfl wing, and scholars are coos' antly tiing refused a linission. The Church of England school managers have th • matter under consideration, and at a meeting held at the latter part of last year, a small commit, er was appointed to consider the cpies(ion, coilec information, and present a scheme to a joint meet- ing. Ihe committee consists of the Rev Morley Stev,nson, Rev Canon Willis, Rev E 11 Tarbuck, Mr J D Buckton, Mr CS Burgess, Mr A J Joley. It has been suggesled that it is desirable for the Committee to present a whole scheme, which will meet. the demands of Warrington for the next ten years. Such a scheme, it is thought, will involve an expenditure of over .ElO,OOO, and it is believed that a general appeal will have a better chance of success ihan if separate appeals are issued ler the parishes effected. The scheme under considera- tion inclu les an enlaroement of St Ann's School (alluded to in Mr Phelps' letter), a new school in St Peter's Parish for boys and girls; classrooms added to All Saints Mission room to form new schools ; a new inlauts school adjoin- ing St James scool, Latchlord ; a cla,sroom added to St Barnahas' School, and I urther accom mod ation in the Fairfield district. Ibis extensive scheme La,- not yet been laid before the pubic, but it is neces- sary that immediate steps should be taken if War rington wishes to be free from a School Board.

PROP3SED NEW THEATRE FOR WARRINGTON. An Important Scheme. We have good ground, for st.ting that negotiations are in progress for acq•airing fr the Warrington Cor- poration the Feathers Hotel (now in course of erection in Bridge-street), and the land adjoining (including that in the rear) for the purpose of erecting a first class heatre. The scheme Is still in emfirvo, and no definite information can be obtained, hut we understand that the promoters are a London company prominently con- nected with tie dramatic or )fession and their desire is t , provide Warrington with a theatre which shall be •qual in appointment and reputation to any existing theatre in the adjoining cities of Manchest!r and Liver- pool. We learn that the erection of the hotel will be proceeded with, and accommodation will be provided to meet the requirements of 1113 various drat class com- panies widen will perform at the theatre. The adjoin- ing land will be the site for the proposed theatre, which we understand, will oost something like £30,000.
